I might have been a bit annoyed about the delay, because we were supposed to be meeting my brother for lunch and he always hated when people were late, but fear? No, not an ounce of it. It's only since we lost Arthur that everything changed. Before that terrible day, a bridge was just a way to get from point A to point B.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges currently preventing you from doing things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it breaks my heart to say it, but yes. Yes, it absolutely is. It’s like my whole world has been shrunk down to a little box, and I just can't seem to find my way out of it. It’s not just an inconvenience, you see; it’s genuinely changing how I live my life, day in and day out. \n\nTake my trips, for example. I used to love traveling. After Martha passed, I still kept up with some of our old routes, driving up the coast, taking little weekend getaways. But now? I can't do it. The thought of encountering one of those big bridges, the ones that span the bays... I just can't bring myself to even plan a trip anymore. I've had to cancel three different outings in the last year alone because I looked at the map and realized there was no way around a major crossing. \n\nAnd it's not just the big trips, either. It’s the everyday things, the things that used to bring me joy. I used to have season tickets to the theater downtown. Oh, I loved going down there, getting dressed up, seeing a good play. But the quickest way into the city from my house is over the suspension bridge, the one near where Arthur... well, you know. I tried finding alternate routes, taking the long way around on the surface streets, but even those have smaller overpasses, and sometimes just the thought of getting trapped on one is enough to make me stay home. So, I gave up the tickets. I just let them go. \n\nEven my volunteer work has suffered. I used to go down to the local historical society twice a week to help catalog their archives. I’ve always had a mind for dates and details, you see, ever since my accounting days. But the society building is across the river. For a while, I tried having my daughter drive me, but I’d be in such a state of panic by the time we got there, I couldn't concentrate on the work. My hands would be shaking too much to handle the old documents. I finally had to tell them I couldn't come in anymore. \n\nIt makes me feel so... so helpless, doctor. I’m an eighty-seven-year-old man, I should be enjoying my retirement, not hiding in my house. I've always been independent, always prided myself on taking care of my own affairs. But now, my days have become so small, so restricted. I have to rely on my daughter or my neighbors to run errands if it involves crossing town, or I just don't go at all. It’s like this fear is a wall that’s been built right down the middle of my life, and I’m stuck on one side of it, watching everything I used to enjoy just slip away.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about bridges, such as their height or the water below, that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it’s... it’s the height, certainly, but it’s more than just being up high. It’s the... the precariousness of it all. You see, back when I was working—I was an accountant, as I might have mentioned, very grounded, very much about numbers and solid facts, things you can count on. I always used to appreciate the engineering of it all. But now... now when I look at those massive pillars and the cables, my mind just goes to the absolute worst-case scenario. \n\nIt’s not even necessarily the water below, though that certainly doesn't help, especially on days when the wind whips up and you can see the whitecaps chopping around down there in the bay. But really, it’s the structure itself. The emptiness underneath it. The rational part of my brain—the part that balanced ledgers for forty years—I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those exact bridges every single day without a hitch. Heavy trucks, buses, families on vacation, they all go over just fine, day in and day out. \n\nBut... but when I see a high span, whether I'm just sitting at my kitchen table tracing a route on a map, or worse, if it suddenly looms up ahead through the windshield while we're driving... oh, goodness. All that logic just completely evaporates. It feels to me as if the whole thing, the concrete, the steel, the road itself, is just waiting to drop away right from under us. \n\nI sit there looking at it, and I can almost feel that stomach-churning sensation of falling before we're even on the ramp. I picture the supports giving way, the cables snapping like... like cheap string. It’s this overwhelming sense of impending doom, like the bridge is a trap just waiting to spring the moment my car tires touch the expansion joint. I know it sounds completely foolish to say out loud to a man of science like yourself, because structurally they are perfectly sound, but the terror in my chest doesn't care about structural integrity, doctor. It just tells me the ground is about to vanish, and I can't shake that feeling no matter how hard I try to reason with myself.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs there a particular part of the bridge-crossing experience that triggers your fear, such as the moment of crossing the center span?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
The center span? Oh, goodness, no. No, doctor, I wish it were just the center span. If it were only the middle, well, maybe I could just close my eyes for a few seconds and have my daughter talk me through it. But it doesn't wait for that. Not at all. Let me explain how it actually happens, because this is really the crux of the matter, the whole reason what brings me in to see you. 

You see, it’s the anticipation. It’s the sheer inevitability of it. We could be miles away from the actual structure. Just last month, for instance, my daughter Sarah was driving me to an appointment across town, and we were on the highway. I saw the green highway sign that said the bridge was two miles ahead. Just seeing that sign, knowing there was no exit between us and the water... that’s when it hits me. 

The very moment a bridge crossing becomes unavoidable, my heart just starts pounding away in my chest like a drum. It’s completely immediate. My hands... well, if I'm the one driving, they go completely damp on the steering wheel, just slick with sweat. If Sarah is driving, I’m gripping the passenger armrest so tightly, my hands are just damp and clammy against the plastic. I feel this... this terrible, overwhelming rush of fear. And doctor, this is all happening before I’m even on the bridge! 

By the time we actually reach the incline, let alone that center span you mentioned, I am already completely consumed by it. It’s the knowing that I have to go over it, that feeling of being trapped on that road. Once you're on that approach, there's no pulling over, no turning around, you see? You are committed. And my mind just cannot tolerate that. So, no, it's not the middle, or the highest point. It's the exact second I realize there is no turning back. That immediate terror before the tires even touch the bridge... that's what I just can't live with anymore.

I know it sounds foolish at my age, eighty-seven years old and I should be past all this, but it's just gotten entirely out of hand. What brings me in is that the moment a bridge crossing becomes unavoidable—and I mean the very second I realize there's no other route, no detour, no way around it—it’s like a switch flips inside me. It's really starting to limit where I can go and what I can do, and I just can't keep living like this, being so terrified before the tires even touch the concrete.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear limited to bridges, or do you experience similar feelings in other high places or enclosed areas?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, that's the strange thing about it. It really is just the bridges. I can go up to the top floor of a tall building—my old accounting firm was on the thirty-second floor, and I could look right out the window, clear across the city, without a second thought. But the moment I see a high span on the map, or if we're driving and I see it looming up ahead through the windshield, all that logic just evaporates. It just vanishes completely. It feels to me, right in the pit of my stomach, as if the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I get this terrible, vivid image of the concrete crumbling, the steel groaning, and the entire structure just giving way the second our tires roll onto it. It's not a fear of heights, you see, it's this absolute certainty that the bridge itself is going to fail, and we're going to go down with it. It’s so specific, and so powerful, I just can't reason my way out of it, no matter how many times I tell myself it's safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear episodes in situations that have nothing to do with bridges?\n\nPatient: No, no, never. Not at all. It's really quite peculiar when you think about it, because I've lived a long life, eighty-seven years, and I've been through my fair share of stressful situations, as anyone my age has. I remember back in '68, the market took a terrible dive, and being an accountant, I was right in the thick of it, working fourteen-hour days, people panicking all around me. But I never felt anything like this. I never felt that sudden, overwhelming terror, that feeling that my heart was going to pound right out of my chest. \n\nEven when I had my hip replaced a few years ago—which was quite an ordeal, let me tell you, the recovery was much harder than I anticipated, and I had to use a walker for months—I was nervous, certainly, but it was a normal kind of nervous. I don't get panic attacks out of the blue while I'm sitting in my armchair reading the morning paper, or watching the news, or having dinner with my daughter. You see, back when I was working—I was an accountant, as I might have mentioned, very grounded, very much about numbers and solid facts, things you can count on. I always used to appreciate the engineering of it all. But now... now when I look at those massive pillars and the cables, my mind just goes to the absolute worst-case scenario. \n\nIt’s not even necessarily the water below, though that certainly doesn't help, especially on days when the wind whips up and you can see the whitecaps chopping around down there in the bay. But really, it’s the structure itself. The emptiness underneath it. The rational part of my brain—the part that balanced ledgers for forty years—I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those exact bridges every single day without a hitch. Heavy trucks, buses, families on vacation, they all go over just fine, day in and day out. \n\nBut... but when I see a high span, whether I'm just sitting at my kitchen table tracing a route on a map, or worse, if it suddenly looms up ahead through the windshield while we're driving... oh, goodness. All that logic just completely evaporates. It feels to me as if the whole thing, the concrete, the steel, the road itself, is just waiting to drop away right from under us. \n\nI sit there looking at it, and I can almost feel that stomach-churning sensation of falling before we're even on the ramp. I picture the supports giving way, the cables snapping like... like cheap string. It’s this overwhelming sense of impending doom, like the bridge is a trap just waiting to spring the moment my car tires touch the expansion joint. I know it sounds completely foolish to say out loud to a man of science like yourself, because structurally they are perfectly sound, but the terror in my chest doesn't care about structural integrity, doctor. It just tells me the ground is about to vanish, and I can't shake that feeling no matter how hard I try to reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular part of the bridge-crossing experience that triggers your fear, such as the moment of crossing the center span?\n\nPatient: The center span? Oh, goodness, no. No, doctor, I wish it were just the center span. If it were only the middle, well, maybe I could just close my eyes for a few seconds and have my daughter talk me through it. But it doesn't wait for that. Not at all. Let me explain how it actually happens, because this is really the crux of the matter, the whole reason what brings me in to see you. \n\nYou see, it’s the anticipation. It’s the sheer inevitability of it. We could be miles away from the actual structure. Just last month, for instance, my daughter Sarah was driving me to an appointment across town, and we were on the highway. I saw the green highway sign that said the bridge was two miles ahead. Just seeing that sign, knowing there was no exit between us and the water... that’s when it hits me. \n\nThe very moment a bridge crossing becomes unavoidable, my heart just starts pounding away in my chest like a drum. It’s completely immediate. My hands... well, if I'm the one driving, they go completely damp on the steering wheel, just slick with sweat. If Sarah is driving, I’m gripping the passenger armrest so tightly, my hands are just damp and clammy against the plastic. I feel this... this terrible, overwhelming rush of fear. And doctor, this is all happening before I’m even on the bridge! \n\nBy the time we actually reach the incline, let alone that center span you mentioned, I am already completely consumed by it. It’s the knowing that I have to go over it, that feeling of being trapped on that road. Once you're on that approach, there's no pulling over, no turning around, you see? You are committed. And my mind just cannot tolerate that. So, no, it's not the middle, or the highest point. It's the exact second I realize there is no turning back. That immediate terror before the tires even touch the bridge... that's what I just can't live with anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"candidates": ["F40.231"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

What brings me in is that the moment a bridge crossing becomes unavoidable—and I mean the very second I realize there's no other route, no detour, no way around it—it’s like a switch flips inside me. My heart starts pounding so hard I can feel it in my ears, just thudding away, and my hands... well, they go completely damp. It's this terrible, overwhelming rush of fear that washes over me, and the worst part is, it happens before I’m even on the bridge. Just the thought of it, just seeing the signs for it or knowing it's coming up on the road ahead... it completely takes over. I used to be able to manage it, somewhat, years ago, but now it's just so severe I can't even think straight when it happens. It's really starting to limit where I can go and what I can do, and I just can't keep living like this, being so terrified before the tires even touch the concrete.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear limited to bridges, or do you experience similar feelings in other high places or enclosed areas?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, that's the strange thing about it. It really is just the bridges. I can go up to the top floor of a tall building—my old accounting firm was on the thirty-second floor, and I could look right out the window, clear across the city, without a second thought. And elevators? Never bothered me. I can ride up and down in those little boxes all day long. But bridges... it’s entirely different. \n\nI know perfectly well, in my logical mind, that thousands of cars cross those bridges every single day. I see them doing it. I know the engineering is sound, I know they're built to withstand wind and weight and all of that. It feels to me, right in the pit of my stomach, as if the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I get this terrible, vivid image of the concrete crumbling, the steel groaning, and the entire structure just giving way the second our tires roll onto it. It's not a fear of heights, you see, it's this absolute certainty that the bridge itself is going to fail, and we're going to go down with it. It’s so specific, and so powerful, I just can't reason my way out of it, no matter how many times I tell myself it's safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear episodes in situations that have nothing to do with bridges?\n\nPatient: No, no, never. Not at all. It's really quite peculiar when you think about it, because I've lived a long life, eighty-seven years, and I've been through my fair share of stressful situations, as anyone my age has. I remember back in '68, the market took a terrible dive, and being an accountant, I was right in the thick of it, working fourteen-hour days, people panicking all around me. But I never felt anything like this. I never felt that sudden, overwhelming terror, that feeling that my heart was going to pound right out of my chest. \n\nEven when I had my hip replaced a few years ago—which was quite an ordeal, let me tell you, the recovery was much harder than I anticipated, and I had to use a walker for months—I was nervous, certainly, but it was a normal kind of nervous. It wasn't this... this complete loss of control. I don't get panic attacks out of the blue while I'm sitting in my armchair reading the morning paper, or watching the news, or having dinner with my daughter. I don't wake up in the middle of the night in a cold sweat unless I know I have to travel the next day and there's a bridge on the itinerary. \n\nIt's entirely, one hundred percent tied to the bridges. If I'm nowhere near a bridge, and I know I don't have to cross one, I'm as calm as a cucumber. I can go to the grocery store, I can go to the bank, I can sit in a crowded restaurant, and I feel absolutely fine. It's just the bridges, but heaven knows, that's more than enough to handle.\n\nDoctor: Has anything significant or traumatic ever happened to you involving a bridge?\n\nPatient: Well, yes and no. We'd known each other for, goodness, it must have been forty years. We met at the firm, actually, both of us junior accountants trying to make our way, and we just hit it off. Our wives were close, our children grew up together... he was like a brother to me, really. \n\nAnyway, it was about two years and five months ago. I remember the timing exactly because it was just after my eighty-fifth birthday. Arthur had come over for a little celebration my daughter put together, and he was in such good spirits. But a few weeks later... well, there was a terrible accident. He was driving on that route, the one that goes right up to the big suspension bridge on the east side of town. It was raining, a really nasty storm, the kind where the wipers just can't keep up and the roads get slick as ice. There was a pile-up just near the approach to the bridge. \n\nArthur was caught right in the middle of it. He didn't make it. And I know the accident happened just before the bridge itself, not right in the middle of the span, but in my mind... they're connected forever now. The route, the bridge, the accident, losing Arthur... it all just tangled together into this awful knot. Before that, I crossed bridges without a second thought. I'd drive over that very same bridge to go visit him at his place in the suburbs! But ever since he died, ever since that awful phone call from his wife... that's when this whole thing started. Two years and five months of this absolute terror, and it all traces back to that day. I just can't separate the bridge from what happened to him, no matter how hard I try to reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the memory of your friend Arthur's accident plays a role in triggering your fear when you see or think about bridges?\n\nPatient: Oh, it absolutely does. It’s... well, it’s like a heavy shadow that just falls over my mind the second the thought enters my head. I’ll be sitting there in the car—my daughter usually drives me on longer trips these days, bless her heart, because at eighty-seven, you know, it's just safer to let her handle the traffic, though I still drive myself to the local grocer now and then. But if we're out, and I realize the route we're on means a bridge crossing has become unavoidable... well, doctor, that right there is exactly what brings me in to see you. \n\nIt's not just a sad memory of Arthur, you see, it's a complete physical takeover. I sit there, drenched in sweat, completely terrified, before our tires have even touched the approach ramp. Goodness, yes. Before the accident, I crossed bridges all the time, and I never felt a thing. Not a single flutter of the heart. I mean, my late wife, Martha—rest her soul—and I, we used to take trips up the coast every autumn. We loved to see the leaves changing, you know, the reds and golds, it was just breathtaking. People were getting out of their cars, leaning on the railings, looking down at the boats. It's only since we lost Arthur that everything changed. My hands would be shaking too much to handle the old documents. I finally had to tell them I couldn't come in anymore. \n\nIt makes me feel so... so helpless, doctor. It’s like this fear is a wall that’s been built right down the middle of my life, and I’m stuck on one side of it, watching everything I used to enjoy just slip away.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about bridges, such as their height or the water below, that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it’s... it’s the height, certainly, but it’s more than just being up high. You see, back when I was working—I was an accountant, as I might have mentioned, very grounded, very much about numbers and solid facts, things you can count on. But now... now when I look at those massive pillars and the cables, my mind just goes to the absolute worst-case scenario. \n\nIt’s not even necessarily the water below, though that certainly doesn't help, especially on days when the wind whips up and you can see the whitecaps chopping around down there in the bay. But really, it’s the structure itself. The emptiness underneath it. Heavy trucks, buses, families on vacation, they all go over just fine, day in and day out. \n\nBut... but when I see a high span, whether I'm just sitting at my kitchen table tracing a route on a map, or worse, if it suddenly looms up ahead through the windshield while we're driving... oh, goodness. All that logic just completely evaporates. It feels to me as if the whole thing, the concrete, the steel, the road itself, is just waiting to drop away right from under us. \n\nI sit there looking at it, and I can almost feel that stomach-churning sensation of falling before we're even on the ramp. I picture the supports giving way, the cables snapping like... like cheap string. It’s this overwhelming sense of impending doom, like the bridge is a trap just waiting to spring the moment my car tires touch the expansion joint. I know it sounds completely foolish to say out loud to a man of science like yourself, because structurally they are perfectly sound, but the terror in my chest doesn't care about structural integrity, doctor. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, as I was touching on just a moment ago, it really does come back to the structure itself, yes. The materials, the height... all of it just combines into this terrible picture in my mind. 

I mean, logically, I know they use reinforced concrete, high-tensile steel cables... I used to read up on these things, you know, back when I found engineering fascinating rather than terrifying. My rational mind—that accountant's brain I mentioned—is perfectly aware that those materials are tested, that they are built to hold up under immense pressure. I know perfectly well that thousands upon thousands of cars, heavy trucks, commuter buses, they all cross those exact bridges every single day without a single incident. It’s a statistical fact, and I have always been a man who respects facts. 

But... well, it’s like a switch flips in my head. The moment I see a high span... even if it's just a line drawn on a map while I'm sitting safely at my kitchen table, or, heaven forbid, if the actual structure is looming up ahead through the windshield while we're on the highway... all that logic just goes right out the window. It doesn't matter to me if it's made of the strongest steel on earth. To me, in that moment, it feels as if the whole thing is just... just waiting to drop away right from under us. 

I look at those massive pillars and I just imagine the concrete crumbling, the steel snapping... it's this absolute, terrifying certainty that the road is going to vanish into thin air. It's so incredibly frustrating because I know the materials are sound, I really do, but my body just reacts like we are driving onto a trapdoor that's about to spring open. I just can't seem to make my brain listen to reason once that structure is in my sights.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... it's this whole situation with bridges. I know it sounds foolish at my age, eighty-seven years old and I should be past all this, but it's just gotten entirely out of hand. What brings me in is that the moment a bridge crossing becomes unavoidable—and I mean the very second I realize there's no other route, no detour, no way around it—it’s like a switch flips inside me. My heart starts pounding so hard I can feel it in my ears, just thudding away, and my hands... well, they go completely damp. If I'm driving, they're sweating all over the steering wheel, and if my daughter is driving me, I'm gripping the armrest so hard my knuckles turn white and my palms are just drenched. It's this terrible, overwhelming rush of fear that washes over me, and the worst part is, it happens before I’m even on the bridge. Just the thought of it, just seeing the signs for it or knowing it's coming up on the road ahead... it completely takes over. I used to be able to manage it, somewhat, years ago, but now it's just so severe I can't even think straight when it happens. It's really starting to limit where I can go and what I can do, and I just can't keep living like this, being so terrified before the tires even touch the concrete.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear limited to bridges, or do you experience similar feelings in other high places or enclosed areas?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, that's the strange thing about it. It really is just the bridges. I can go up to the top floor of a tall building—my old accounting firm was on the thirty-second floor, and I could look right out the window, clear across the city, without a second thought. And elevators? Never bothered me. I can ride up and down in those little boxes all day long. But bridges... it’s entirely different. \n\nI know perfectly well, in my logical mind, that thousands of cars cross those bridges every single day. I see them doing it. I know the engineering is sound, I know they're built to withstand wind and weight and all of that. I read an article once about the suspension cables and how much tension they can hold—fascinating stuff, really. But the moment I see a high span on the map, or if we're driving and I see it looming up ahead through the windshield, all that logic just evaporates. It just vanishes completely. It feels to me, right in the pit of my stomach, as if the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I get this terrible, vivid image of the concrete crumbling, the steel groaning, and the entire structure just giving way the second our tires roll onto it. It's not a fear of heights, you see, it's this absolute certainty that the bridge itself is going to fail, and we're going to go down with it. It’s so specific, and so powerful, I just can't reason my way out of it, no matter how many times I tell myself it's safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear episodes in situations that have nothing to do with bridges?\n\nPatient: No, no, never. Not at all. It's really quite peculiar when you think about it, because I've lived a long life, eighty-seven years, and I've been through my fair share of stressful situations, as anyone my age has. I remember back in '68, the market took a terrible dive, and being an accountant, I was right in the thick of it, working fourteen-hour days, people panicking all around me. But I never felt anything like this. I never felt that sudden, overwhelming terror, that feeling that my heart was going to pound right out of my chest. \n\nEven when I had my hip replaced a few years ago—which was quite an ordeal, let me tell you, the recovery was much harder than I anticipated, and I had to use a walker for months—I was nervous, certainly, but it was a normal kind of nervous. It wasn't this... this complete loss of control. I don't get panic attacks out of the blue while I'm sitting in my armchair reading the morning paper, or watching the news, or having dinner with my daughter. I don't wake up in the middle of the night in a cold sweat unless I know I have to travel the next day and there's a bridge on the itinerary. \n\nIt's entirely, one hundred percent tied to the bridges. If I'm nowhere near a bridge, and I know I don't have to cross one, I'm as calm as a cucumber. I can go to the grocery store, I can go to the bank, I can sit in a crowded restaurant, and I feel absolutely fine. It's only when that specific threat—that high span of concrete and steel—enters the picture that everything just falls apart. So no, doctor, there are no other situations. It's just the bridges, but heaven knows, that's more than enough to handle.\n\nDoctor: Has anything significant or traumatic ever happened to you involving a bridge?\n\nPatient: Well, yes and no. It wasn't me, you see, not directly. I wasn't the one on the bridge when it happened, but... oh, it just breaks my heart to even talk about it, really. It was my dear friend, Arthur. We'd known each other for, goodness, it must have been forty years. We met at the firm, actually, both of us junior accountants trying to make our way, and we just hit it off. Our wives were close, our children grew up together... he was like a brother to me, really. \n\nAnyway, it was about two years and five months ago. I remember the timing exactly because it was just after my eighty-fifth birthday. Arthur had come over for a little celebration my daughter put together, and he was in such good spirits. But a few weeks later... well, there was a terrible accident. He was driving on that route, the one that goes right up to the big suspension bridge on the east side of town. It was raining, a really nasty storm, the kind where the wipers just can't keep up and the roads get slick as ice. There was a pile-up just near the approach to the bridge. \n\nArthur was caught right in the middle of it. He didn't make it. And I know the accident happened just before the bridge itself, not right in the middle of the span, but in my mind... they're connected forever now. The route, the bridge, the accident, losing Arthur... it all just tangled together into this awful knot. Before that, I crossed bridges without a second thought. I'd drive over that very same bridge to go visit him at his place in the suburbs! But ever since he died, ever since that awful phone call from his wife... that's when this whole thing started. Two years and five months of this absolute terror, and it all traces back to that day. I just can't separate the bridge from what happened to him, no matter how hard I try to reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the memory of your friend Arthur's accident plays a role in triggering your fear when you see or think about bridges?\n\nPatient: Oh, it absolutely does. It’s... well, it’s like a heavy shadow that just falls over my mind the second the thought enters my head. I’ll be sitting there in the car—my daughter usually drives me on longer trips these days, bless her heart, because at eighty-seven, you know, it's just safer to let her handle the traffic, though I still drive myself to the local grocer now and then. The moment I see a high span... even if it's just a line drawn on a map while I'm sitting safely at my kitchen table, or, heaven forbid, if the actual structure is looming up ahead through the windshield while we're on the highway... all that logic just goes right out the window. It doesn't matter to me if it's made of the strongest steel on earth. To me, in that moment, it feels as if the whole thing is just... just waiting to drop away right from under us. \n\nI look at those massive pillars and I just imagine the concrete crumbling, the steel snapping... it's this absolute, terrifying certainty that the road is going to vanish into thin air. It's so incredibly frustrating because I know the materials are sound, I really do, but my body just reacts like we are driving onto a trapdoor that's about to spring open. I just can't seem to make my brain listen to reason once that structure is in my sights.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It's not a fear of heights, you see, it's this absolute certainty that the bridge itself is going to fail, and we're going to go down with it. It’s so specific, and so powerful, I just can't reason my way out of it, no matter how many times I tell myself it's safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear episodes in situations that have nothing to do with bridges?\n\nPatient: No, no, never. Not at all. It's really quite peculiar when you think about it, because I've lived a long life, eighty-seven years, and I've been through my fair share of stressful situations, as anyone my age has. I remember back in '68, the market took a terrible dive, and being an accountant, I was right in the thick of it, working fourteen-hour days, people panicking all around me. But I never felt anything like this. I never felt that sudden, overwhelming terror, that feeling that my heart was going to pound right out of my chest. \n\nEven when I had my hip replaced a few years ago—which was quite an ordeal, let me tell you, the recovery was much harder than I anticipated, and I had to use a walker for months—I was nervous, certainly, but it was a normal kind of nervous. I don't get panic attacks out of the blue while I'm sitting in my armchair reading the morning paper, or watching the news, or having dinner with my daughter. I can go to the grocery store, I can go to the bank, I can sit in a crowded restaurant, and I feel absolutely fine. It was raining, a really nasty storm, the kind where the wipers just can't keep up and the roads get slick as ice. There was a pile-up just near the approach to the bridge. \n\nArthur was caught right in the middle of it. He didn't make it. And I know the accident happened just before the bridge itself, not right in the middle of the span, but in my mind... they're connected forever now. I'd drive over that very same bridge to go visit him at his place in the suburbs! But ever since he died, ever since that awful phone call from his wife... that's when this whole thing started. It’s like this fear is a wall that’s been built right down the middle of my life, and I’m stuck on one side of it, watching everything I used to enjoy just slip away.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about bridges, such as their height or the water below, that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it’s... it’s the height, certainly, but it’s more than just being up high. But now... now when I look at those massive pillars and the cables, my mind just goes to the absolute worst-case scenario. \n\nIt’s not even necessarily the water below, though that certainly doesn't help, especially on days when the wind whips up and you can see the whitecaps chopping around down there in the bay. But really, it’s the structure itself. The emptiness underneath it. The rational part of my brain—the part that balanced ledgers for forty years—I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those exact bridges every single day without a hitch. Heavy trucks, buses, families on vacation, they all go over just fine, day in and day out. \n\nBut... but when I see a high span, whether I'm just sitting at my kitchen table tracing a route on a map, or worse, if it suddenly looms up ahead through the windshield while we're driving... oh, goodness. All that logic just completely evaporates. It feels to me as if the whole thing, the concrete, the steel, the road itself, is just waiting to drop away right from under us. \n\nI sit there looking at it, and I can almost feel that stomach-churning sensation of falling before we're even on the ramp. I picture the supports giving way, the cables snapping like... like cheap string. It’s this overwhelming sense of impending doom, like the bridge is a trap just waiting to spring the moment my car tires touch the expansion joint. I know it sounds completely foolish to say out loud to a man of science like yourself, because structurally they are perfectly sound, but the terror in my chest doesn't care about structural integrity, doctor. It just tells me the ground is about to vanish, and I can't shake that feeling no matter how hard I try to reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular part of the bridge-crossing experience that triggers your fear, such as the moment of crossing the center span?\n\nPatient: The center span? I used to read up on these things, you know, back when I found engineering fascinating rather than terrifying. I know it sounds completely foolish to say out loud to a man of science like yourself, because structurally they are perfectly sound, but the terror in my chest doesn't care about structural integrity, doctor. It just tells me the ground is about to vanish, and I can't shake that feeling no matter how hard I try to reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular part of the bridge-crossing experience that triggers your fear, such as the moment of crossing the center span?\n\nPatient: The center span? Oh, goodness, no. No, doctor, I wish it were just the center span. If it were only the middle, well, maybe I could just close my eyes for a few seconds and have my daughter talk me through it. But it doesn't wait for that. Not at all. Let me explain how it actually happens, because this is really the crux of the matter, the whole reason what brings me in to see you. \n\nYou see, it’s the anticipation. It’s the sheer inevitability of it. We could be miles away from the actual structure. Just last month, for instance, my daughter Sarah was driving me to an appointment across town, and we were on the highway. I saw the green highway sign that said the bridge was two miles ahead. Just seeing that sign, knowing there was no exit between us and the water... that’s when it hits me. \n\nThe very moment a bridge crossing becomes unavoidable, my heart just starts pounding away in my chest like a drum. And doctor, this is all happening before I’m even on the bridge! \n\nBy the time we actually reach the incline, let alone that center span you mentioned, I am already completely consumed by it. It’s the knowing that I have to go over it, that feeling of being trapped on that road. Once you're on that approach, there's no pulling over, no turning around, you see? You are committed. That immediate terror before the tires even touch the bridge... that's what I just can't live with anymore.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the structure of bridges that triggers your fear, such as the height or the material used?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, as I was touching on just a moment ago, it really does come back to the structure itself, yes. The materials, the height... all of it just combines into this terrible picture in my mind. \n\nI mean, logically, I know they use reinforced concrete, high-tensile steel cables... I used to read up on these things, you know, back when I found engineering fascinating rather than terrifying. My rational mind—that accountant's brain I mentioned—is perfectly aware that those materials are tested, that they are built to hold up under immense pressure. I know perfectly well that thousands upon thousands of cars, heavy trucks, commuter buses, they all cross those exact bridges every single day without a single incident. It doesn't matter to me if it's made of the strongest steel on earth. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, it has, doctor. It most certainly has, and that... well, that might be the hardest part of all this to swallow. You see, I was always a very independent man. Always the one organizing things, making the schedules, driving everyone around. But now? I’ve had to give up so much. 

I’ve had to give up trips we used to take—my daughter Sarah and I used to drive up the coast every summer, a beautiful drive, but you have to cross three major inlets to get there, so that's entirely out of the question now. And my theater outings! I used to have season tickets to the downtown playhouse. My old friend Arthur and I went for years, every other Thursday like clockwork. But getting downtown means taking the interstate over the river, and I just... I had to call Arthur and tell him to find someone else to take my ticket. I couldn't bear the drive. 

And it’s not just the leisure activities, either. I had volunteer commitments I really used to count on to keep my mind sharp. After I retired from the firm, I started doing pro bono tax preparation for seniors at the community center on the east side. I loved it, doctor. It gave me a real sense of purpose, a way to use my skills. But... well, the only practical way there is over that massive suspension bridge. I had to call the director and step down. I felt like I was completely abandoning them. 

Because of all this avoiding, my days have just become... smaller. That's the only word for it. My whole world is shrinking down to just a few safe square miles around my house. And the strain it puts on my family... well, I’m now relying on others for plans I used to handle entirely by myself. If I want to see my grandchildren, or even just go to a specific store, I have to ask Sarah to drive me. And not just drive me, but I have to make her take these ridiculous, convoluted, hour-long detours just to stay on flat ground and avoid any high spans. I know it frustrates her, even if she's too kind to admit it. It makes me feel like such a terrible burden to the people I love, just sitting here in my shrinking little world, unable to even drive across town to see a play with an old friend.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... it's this whole situation with bridges. I know it sounds foolish at my age, eighty-seven years old and I should be past all this, but it's just gotten entirely out of hand. What brings me in is that the moment a bridge crossing becomes unavoidable—and I mean the very second I realize there's no other route, no detour, no way around it—it’s like a switch flips inside me. My heart starts pounding so hard I can feel it in my ears, just thudding away, and my hands... well, they go completely damp. If I'm driving, they're sweating all over the steering wheel, and if my daughter is driving me, I'm gripping the armrest so hard my knuckles turn white and my palms are just drenched. Just the thought of it, just seeing the signs for it or knowing it's coming up on the road ahead... it completely takes over. I used to be able to manage it, somewhat, years ago, but now it's just so severe I can't even think straight when it happens. It's really starting to limit where I can go and what I can do, and I just can't keep living like this, being so terrified before the tires even touch the concrete.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear limited to bridges, or do you experience similar feelings in other high places or enclosed areas?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, that's the strange thing about it. It really is just the bridges. I can go up to the top floor of a tall building—my old accounting firm was on the thirty-second floor, and I could look right out the window, clear across the city, without a second thought. And elevators? Never bothered me. I can ride up and down in those little boxes all day long. But bridges... it’s entirely different. \n\nI know perfectly well, in my logical mind, that thousands of cars cross those bridges every single day. I see them doing it. I read an article once about the suspension cables and how much tension they can hold—fascinating stuff, really. But the moment I see a high span on the map, or if we're driving and I see it looming up ahead through the windshield, all that logic just evaporates. It just vanishes completely. It feels to me, right in the pit of my stomach, as if the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I get this terrible, vivid image of the concrete crumbling, the steel groaning, and the entire structure just giving way the second our tires roll onto it. It's not a fear of heights, you see, it's this absolute certainty that the bridge itself is going to fail, and we're going to go down with it. It’s so specific, and so powerful, I just can't reason my way out of it, no matter how many times I tell myself it's safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear episodes in situations that have nothing to do with bridges?\n\nPatient: No, no, never. Not at all. It's really quite peculiar when you think about it, because I've lived a long life, eighty-seven years, and I've been through my fair share of stressful situations, as anyone my age has. I remember back in '68, the market took a terrible dive, and being an accountant, I was right in the thick of it, working fourteen-hour days, people panicking all around me. But I never felt anything like this. I never felt that sudden, overwhelming terror, that feeling that my heart was going to pound right out of my chest. \n\nEven when I had my hip replaced a few years ago—which was quite an ordeal, let me tell you, the recovery was much harder than I anticipated, and I had to use a walker for months—I was nervous, certainly, but it was a normal kind of nervous. It wasn't this... this complete loss of control. I don't get panic attacks out of the blue while I'm sitting in my armchair reading the morning paper, or watching the news, or having dinner with my daughter. I don't wake up in the middle of the night in a cold sweat unless I know I have to travel the next day and there's a bridge on the itinerary. \n\nIt's entirely, one hundred percent tied to the bridges. If I'm nowhere near a bridge, and I know I don't have to cross one, I'm as calm as a cucumber. I can go to the grocery store, I can go to the bank, I can sit in a crowded restaurant, and I feel absolutely fine. It's only when that specific threat—that high span of concrete and steel—enters the picture that everything just falls apart. So no, doctor, there are no other situations. It's just the bridges, but heaven knows, that's more than enough to handle.\n\nDoctor: Has anything significant or traumatic ever happened to you involving a bridge?\n\nPatient: Well, yes and no. It wasn't me, you see, not directly. I wasn't the one on the bridge when it happened, but... oh, it just breaks my heart to even talk about it, really. It was my dear friend, Arthur. We'd known each other for, goodness, it must have been forty years. We met at the firm, actually, both of us junior accountants trying to make our way, and we just hit it off. Our wives were close, our children grew up together... he was like a brother to me, really. \n\nAnyway, it was about two years and five months ago. I remember the timing exactly because it was just after my eighty-fifth birthday. Arthur had come over for a little celebration my daughter put together, and he was in such good spirits. But a few weeks later... well, there was a terrible accident. He was driving on that route, the one that goes right up to the big suspension bridge on the east side of town. It was raining, a really nasty storm, the kind where the wipers just can't keep up and the roads get slick as ice. There was a pile-up just near the approach to the bridge. \n\nArthur was caught right in the middle of it. He didn't make it. And I know the accident happened just before the bridge itself, not right in the middle of the span, but in my mind... they're connected forever now. The route, the bridge, the accident, losing Arthur... it all just tangled together into this awful knot. Before that, I crossed bridges without a second thought. I'd drive over that very same bridge to go visit him at his place in the suburbs! But ever since he died, ever since that awful phone call from his wife... that's when this whole thing started. Two years and five months of this absolute terror, and it all traces back to that day. I just can't separate the bridge from what happened to him, no matter how hard I try to reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the memory of your friend Arthur's accident plays a role in triggering your fear when you see or think about bridges?\n\nPatient: Oh, it absolutely does. It’s... well, it’s like a heavy shadow that just falls over my mind the second the thought enters my head. I’ll be sitting there in the car—my daughter usually drives me on longer trips these days, bless her heart, because at eighty-seven, you know, it's just safer to let her handle the traffic, though I still drive myself to the local grocer now and then. But if we're out, and I realize the route we're on means a bridge crossing has become unavoidable... well, doctor, that right there is exactly what brings me in to see you. \n\nIt's not just a sad memory of Arthur, you see, it's a complete physical takeover. The very moment I know we have no choice but to cross one, my heart just starts pounding. I mean pounding so violently I can actually hear it rushing in my ears. If I'm sitting in the passenger seat, I'll be white-knuckling the armrest, or if I happen to be the one behind the steering wheel, my palms get so slick with cold sweat I feel like I can barely hold on. I feel this... this terrible, overwhelming rush of fear, this absolute spike of terror, and all of this happens before I’m even on the bridge! \n\nJust the realization that we can't turn back, that the bridge is coming up and Arthur's accident is flashing through my mind... it triggers this avalanche in my body. Goodness, yes. Before the accident, I crossed bridges all the time, and I never felt a thing. Not a single flutter of the heart. I mean, my late wife, Martha—rest her soul—and I, we used to take trips up the coast every autumn. We loved to see the leaves changing, you know, the reds and golds, it was just breathtaking. And to get there, we had to cross some magnificent bridges, those really long ones that span across wide bays and inlets. I would drive, and Martha would have her camera out, snapping pictures right through the windshield. I never felt anxious. I never felt afraid. I was just focused on the road, enjoying the scenery and the company. \n\nAnd even just around town, going to Arthur's place, like I mentioned, or running errands... bridges were just roads over water or a valley to me. Nothing more. People were getting out of their cars, leaning on the railings, looking down at the boats. I might have been a bit annoyed about the delay, because we were supposed to be meeting my brother for lunch and he always hated when people were late, but fear? No, not an ounce of it. If I want to see my grandchildren, or even just go to a specific store, I have to ask Sarah to drive me.
